- **Score**: 7/10

### Enhancing Zero-Shot Image Recognition in Vision-Language Models through Human-like Concept Guidance
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.15886v1)
- **Authors**: Hui Liu, Wenya Wang, Kecheng Chen, Jie Liu, Yibing Liu, Tiexin Qin, Peisong He, Xinghao Jiang, Haoliang Li
- **Abstract**: In zero-shot image recognition tasks, humans demonstrate remarkable flexibility in classifying unseen categories by composing known simpler concepts. However, existing vision-language models (VLMs), despite achieving significant progress through large-scale natural language supervision, often underperform in real-world applications because of sub-optimal prompt engineering and the inability to adapt effectively to target classes. To address these issues, we propose a Concept-guided Human-like Bayesian Reasoning (CHBR) framework. Grounded in Bayes' theorem, CHBR models the concept used in human image recognition as latent variables and formulates this task by summing across potential concepts, weighted by a prior distribution and a likelihood function. To tackle the intractable computation over an infinite concept space, we introduce an importance sampling algorithm that iteratively prompts large language models (LLMs) to generate discriminative concepts, emphasizing inter-class differences. We further propose three heuristic approaches involving Average Likelihood, Confidence Likelihood, and Test Time Augmentation (TTA) Likelihood, which dynamically refine the combination of concepts based on the test image. Extensive evaluations across fifteen datasets demonstrate that CHBR consistently outperforms existing state-of-the-art zero-shot generalization methods.
- **Summary**: Okay, I will provide a summary and a critical evaluation of the paper "Enhancing Zero-Shot Image Recognition in Vision-Language Models through Human-like Concept Guidance."

**Summary**

The paper proposes a novel framework called Concept-guided Human-like Bayesian Reasoning (CHBR) to improve zero-shot image recognition in Vision-Language Models (VLMs). The core idea is to mimic how humans recognize images by composing known concepts. CHBR models the concept used in human image recognition as latent variables and formulates the task as summing across potential concepts, weighted by a prior distribution and a likelihood function.  The method addresses the computational intractability of searching over an infinite concept space by using an importance sampling algorithm that leverages Large Language Models (LLMs) to generate discriminative concepts. Further, it proposes three heuristic approaches (Average Likelihood, Confidence Likelihood, and TTA Likelihood) to dynamically refine the combination of concepts based on the test image.  The authors demonstrate through extensive experiments on fifteen datasets that CHBR outperforms existing state-of-the-art zero-shot generalization methods.

**Critical Evaluation**

*   **Novelty:** The paper introduces a reasonably novel approach to zero-shot image recognition. While the idea of using concepts is not entirely new, the way CHBR integrates it within a Bayesian framework using LLMs for concept generation and discriminative importance sampling is a unique contribution.  The concept of modelling the problem through a latent variable framework that connects human perception and existing VLMs is a clever angle. The three proposed likelihood functions, although heuristic, offer practical ways to refine concept importance at test time. The connection with Monte Carlo methods via the importance sampling is not exactly revolutionary, but it is well justified and applied in a meaningful way.

*   **Significance:**  Zero-shot learning is a significant problem in computer vision, as it allows models to recognize new categories without requiring explicit training data.  Improving the performance of VLMs in this area has broad implications for real-world applications.  If the method is robust (as suggested by the experiments), it could reduce the need for task-specific fine-tuning and enable more adaptable and generalizable vision systems. The paper tackles the common problem of sub-optimal prompting, which often plagues VLMs. CHBR offers a more structured approach to concept selection and utilization.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Sound Theoretical Foundation:** Grounding the method in Bayes' theorem provides a solid theoretical basis.
    *   **Effective Use of LLMs:**  The clever use of LLMs for concept generation addresses a critical challenge of automatically identifying relevant concepts.
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The evaluation across fifteen datasets is thorough and helps demonstrate the generalizability of the approach.
    *   **Adaptive Nature:**  The three likelihood methods effectively adapt the concept combination to specific test images.
    *   **Good discussion of computational cost:** A good discussion of computational resources for the proposed method is provided.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Heuristic Likelihoods:** The likelihood functions, while practical, are largely heuristic and lack a deeper theoretical justification. This could potentially limit the robustness of the method in certain scenarios.
    *   **Dependence on LLMs:** The performance of CHBR is inherently tied to the capabilities of the underlying LLMs used for concept generation. Improvements in LLMs would likely translate to improvements in CHBR, but it also means that the method is vulnerable to limitations and biases present in LLMs. The use of GPT-4 also limits the reproducibility for groups lacking resources.
    *   **Limited Insights into Concept Importance:**  While the paper discusses concept selection, it provides limited insights into *why* certain concepts are more effective than others. A deeper analysis of the learned concept priors could provide valuable insights for future research.
    *   **Still Requires Fine-tuning (TTA Likelihood):** In particular, with TTA Likelihood, the model requires an optimization step during the inference, which makes it computationally intensive. A deeper study should have investigated the trade-off between performance and increased computational cost.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence future research in zero-shot learning, particularly in the areas of concept-based reasoning and leveraging LLMs for knowledge injection into VLMs.  The CHBR framework could serve as a foundation for developing more sophisticated and adaptive zero-shot learning models.

**Justification for Score:**

I am assigning a score of **7** to this paper. While the paper does not present a radical breakthrough, it provides a novel and well-executed approach to zero-shot image recognition. The integration of LLMs and a Bayesian framework is a meaningful contribution, and the comprehensive evaluation demonstrates the effectiveness of the proposed method. The weaknesses, such as the reliance on heuristic likelihoods and dependence on LLMs, are limitations that future research can address. It is a valuable contribution with clear potential to influence further research in this area.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### Parameters vs. Context: Fine-Grained Control of Knowledge Reliance in Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.15888v1)
- **Authors**: Baolong Bi, Shenghua Liu, Yiwei Wang, Yilong Xu, Junfeng Fang, Lingrui Mei, Xueqi Cheng
- **Abstract**: Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) mitigates hallucinations in Large Language Models (LLMs) by integrating external knowledge. However, conflicts between parametric knowledge and retrieved context pose challenges, particularly when retrieved information is unreliable or the model's internal knowledge is outdated. In such cases, LLMs struggle to determine whether to rely more on their own parameters or the conflicted context. To address this, we propose **CK-PLUG**, a plug-and-play method for controlling LLMs' reliance on parametric and contextual knowledge. We introduce a novel knowledge consistency metric, Confidence Gain, which detects knowledge conflicts by measuring entropy shifts in token probability distributions after context insertion. CK-PLUG then enables fine-grained control over knowledge preference by adjusting the probability distribution of tokens with negative confidence gain through a single tuning parameter. Experiments demonstrate CK-PLUG's ability to significantly regulate knowledge reliance in counterfactual RAG scenarios while maintaining generation fluency and knowledge accuracy. For instance, on Llama3-8B, memory recall (MR) of RAG response can be adjusted within a broad range (9.9%-71.9%), compared to the baseline of 42.1%. Moreover, CK-PLUG supports adaptive control based on the model's confidence in both internal and external knowledge, achieving consistent performance improvements across various general RAG tasks. Our code is available at: $\href{https://github.com/byronBBL/CK-PLUG}{\text{this https URL}}$.
- **Summary**: The paper introduces CK-PLUG, a plug-and-play method for controlling the knowledge reliance of Large Language Models (LLMs) in Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) systems. It addresses the challenge of conflicts between parametric knowledge and retrieved context, where LLMs struggle to prioritize one over the other. CK-PLUG employs a novel knowledge consistency metric, Confidence Gain (CG), to detect conflicts by measuring entropy shifts in token probability distributions after context insertion. It then enables fine-grained control over knowledge preference by adjusting the probability distribution of tokens with negative confidence gain through a single tuning parameter. Experiments demonstrate CK-PLUG's ability to regulate knowledge reliance in counterfactual RAG scenarios, maintain generation fluency and knowledge accuracy, and achieve consistent performance improvements across various general RAG tasks.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Strengths:**

*   **Addresses a Significant Problem:** The paper tackles a critical issue in RAG systems: resolving conflicts between parametric knowledge and retrieved context. This is a well-recognized problem that hinders the reliability and trustworthiness of RAG-generated outputs.
*   **Novel Approach:** The introduction of Confidence Gain as a metric for detecting knowledge conflicts is a novel idea and appears to be effective in identifying potentially problematic tokens.
*   **Plug-and-Play Design:** The plug-and-play nature of CK-PLUG is a significant advantage, as it allows for easy integration with existing LLMs without requiring retraining or architectural modifications.
*   **Fine-Grained Control:** The ability to control knowledge preference at the token level offers a level of granularity that is lacking in many existing approaches.
*   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The paper provides a comprehensive evaluation of CK-PLUG across various datasets, LLMs, and RAG scenarios, demonstrating its effectiveness in controlling knowledge reliance and improving generation quality.
*   **Adaptive Adjustment:** The inclusion of an adaptive method for tuning the parameter (alpha) based on model confidence further strengthens the practical applicability.
*   **Code Availability:** The availability of the code promotes reproducibility and facilitates further research in this area.
*  **Case Study and Ablation Study:** Provides insights that validates each aspect of the CK-PLUG.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Reliance on Entropy:** The reliance on entropy as a measure of uncertainty may be sensitive to the specific architecture and training of the LLM. Entropy may not always accurately reflect the model's confidence in its predictions. Additional metrics could've further solidify their conclusions.
*   **Limited Scope of Evaluation:** While the evaluation is comprehensive, it primarily focuses on question-answering tasks. It would be beneficial to evaluate CK-PLUG on a broader range of RAG applications, such as text summarization and dialogue generation.
*   **Parameter Sensitivity:** Although there's an adaptive method, the tuning parameter 'a' might be difficult to set appropriately for all applications without careful experimentation. Further investigation on the parameterization process would be beneficial to the community.
*   **Lack of Detailed Analysis of the Limitations:** While the paper identifies QWEN as an exception, the limitations are not discussed in detail.

**Novelty and Significance:**

The paper makes a significant contribution to the field of RAG by providing a novel and practical method for controlling knowledge reliance in LLMs. The introduction of Confidence Gain and the plug-and-play design of CK-PLUG offer a valuable approach for resolving conflicts between parametric knowledge and retrieved context. The comprehensive evaluation demonstrates the effectiveness of the proposed method and its potential for improving the reliability and trustworthiness of RAG-generated outputs. However, improvements with limitations with the tuning parameters should be carefully investigated and discussed in detail.

**Potential Influence:**

CK-PLUG has the potential to influence the development of more reliable and trustworthy RAG systems. Its plug-and-play design and fine-grained control over knowledge preference make it a valuable tool for researchers and practitioners working with LLMs. The paper's findings may also inspire further research on methods for detecting and resolving knowledge conflicts in RAG systems.

**Score: 8**

**Rationale:**

CK-PLUG addresses a significant and well-recognized problem in RAG systems with a novel and practical solution. The use of Confidence Gain and the plug-and-play design are key strengths. However, the potential sensitivity of the entropy-based metric and the limited scope of evaluation prevent it from achieving a higher score. It's also very important to note that although they offer an adaptive method for tuning, it might still be difficult to implement the parameterization in other use-cases. Overall, it is a significant contribution but could benefit from more robustness and clarity concerning real-world deployment of the parameter tuning and a more detailed analysis of the limitations of certain models.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 8/10

### Time After Time: Deep-Q Effect Estimation for Interventions on When and What to do
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.15890v1)
- **Authors**: Yoav Wald, Mark Goldstein, Yonathan Efroni, Wouter A. C. van Amsterdam, Rajesh Ranganath
- **Abstract**: Problems in fields such as healthcare, robotics, and finance requires reasoning about the value both of what decision or action to take and when to take it. The prevailing hope is that artificial intelligence will support such decisions by estimating the causal effect of policies such as how to treat patients or how to allocate resources over time. However, existing methods for estimating the effect of a policy struggle with \emph{irregular time}. They either discretize time, or disregard the effect of timing policies. We present a new deep-Q algorithm that estimates the effect of both when and what to do called Earliest Disagreement Q-Evaluation (EDQ). EDQ makes use of recursion for the Q-function that is compatible with flexible sequence models, such as transformers. EDQ provides accurate estimates under standard assumptions. We validate the approach through experiments on survival time and tumor growth tasks.
- **Summary**: Okay, I'll provide a summary, a critical evaluation, and a novelty/significance score for the paper "Time After Time: Deep-Q Effect Estimation for Interventions on When and What to do".

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Earliest Disagreement Q-Evaluation (EDQ), a novel deep-Q learning-based algorithm for estimating the causal effect of interventions in sequential decision-making scenarios with irregularly spaced time points.  Unlike existing methods that discretize time or disregard the effect of timing, EDQ leverages recursion and is compatible with flexible sequence models like transformers. EDQ estimates the effect of both *when* and *what* action to take, providing more accurate estimates under standard causal assumptions. The authors validate the approach on survival time and tumor growth tasks, demonstrating its advantage over baselines that rely on discretization. The method directly uses observed data in a model-free setting, updating the Q-function based on the first point in time when the observed trajectory differs from the counterfactual trajectory under a given target policy. It includes proofs of concept and causal validity.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Addresses a relevant and challenging problem:** The paper tackles a significant limitation of existing off-policy evaluation (OPE) methods: handling irregularly spaced time data in sequential decision-making. This is crucial for applications like healthcare, where events and interventions occur at non-uniform intervals.
    *   **Novelty in Approach:** The proposed EDQ algorithm demonstrates a novel and intuitive way to tackle sequential treatment effect estimation, directly learning counterfactuals with dynamic programming. By focusing on the *earliest disagreement time*, it avoids the need for fixed time discretization while retaining a model-free approach (using only observed data for direct regressions).
    *   **Theoretical Justification:** The paper provides theoretical guarantees, proving that EDQ is an empirical estimator of the correct policy value under standard causal assumptions. This adds rigor and provides confidence in the method's validity. The link to graphical criteria and local independence is strong.
    *   **Empirical Validation:**  The experiments on survival time and tumor growth simulation tasks showcase the effectiveness of EDQ compared to baselines, particularly in settings where discretization is problematic. The demonstration of a transformer-based implementation is significant, highlighting the method's compatibility with modern deep learning architectures.
    *   **Clarity:** The paper is well-written and structured, making the complex concepts relatively accessible. The inclusion of a graphical summary of EDQ is helpful.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Real-World Data Validation:** The experiments are conducted primarily on simulated data. While the simulations are relevant, validation on real-world datasets, such as electronic health records (EHR), would significantly strengthen the impact of the paper.
    *   **Scalability of Experiments and Reproducibility:** There is no information concerning the time necessary to optimize a model and implement the algorithm. This limits the ability to understand the full implications of the paper and compare this algorithm against existing benchmarks.
    *   **Practical Considerations on Time-series and Data:** There are no discussions on how the model is made robust to different patterns and trajectories in the dataset. No ablation studies are conducted to understand which parts of the code are most successful. This limits the confidence on the model's wider deployment.
    *   **Assumption of Ignorability:** Like many causal inference methods, EDQ relies on the assumption of ignorability (no unobserved confounders). While the authors acknowledge this and link their assumptions to existing literature, the potential impact of unobserved confounding in real-world applications should be discussed more explicitly. It may be difficult to satisfy in healthcare with large variability.
    *   **Lack of Policy Optimization Discussion:** The paper focuses on off-policy *evaluation*. Extending the method to policy *optimization*, learning the optimal treatment policy directly, would significantly increase its practical relevance.
    *   **Comparison to Existing Work:** The discussion of related work does highlight some existing OPE methods but is not comprehensive. This makes it more difficult to understand the paper's true innovation.

*   **Significance:**

    *   **Contribution to OPE:** EDQ provides a practical and theoretically sound approach for OPE in settings with irregular time data, an area that is currently underserved.
    *   **Potential Impact on Healthcare:** The method holds promise for improving treatment decision support systems in healthcare and other domains where the timing of interventions is critical.

**Overall:**

The paper makes a significant contribution by introducing a novel and well-justified algorithm for OPE with irregular time data. The approach is theoretically sound and shows promising results in simulations. The main limitation is the lack of real-world data validation, policy optimization and concerns regarding scalability and reproducibility of results. While the current work makes a strong step, further real-world deployment, implementation, and discussion around assumptions and scalability would be welcome.

**Score: 7.5**

**Rationale:**

The score reflects the paper's significant novelty in tackling a challenging problem, the theoretical justification, and the promising empirical results. However, the dependence on simulated data, the lack of extensive experimentation, the limited consideration of policy optimization, and some minor deficiencies in the discussion of related work prevent it from receiving a higher score. Overall, the paper presents a good solution with a strong framework but further investigation to improve deployment, implementation, and performance is necessary.

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### CONTHER: Human-Like Contextual Robot Learning via Hindsight Experience Replay and Transformers without Expert Demonstrations
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.15895v1)
- **Authors**: Maria Makarova, Qian Liu, Dzmitry Tsetserukou
- **Abstract**: This paper presents CONTHER, a novel reinforcement learning algorithm designed to efficiently and rapidly train robotic agents for goal-oriented manipulation tasks and obstacle avoidance. The algorithm uses a modified replay buffer inspired by the Hindsight Experience Replay (HER) approach to artificially populate experience with successful trajectories, effectively addressing the problem of sparse reward scenarios and eliminating the need to manually collect expert demonstrations. The developed algorithm proposes a Transformer-based architecture to incorporate the context of previous states, allowing the agent to perform a deeper analysis and make decisions in a manner more akin to human learning. The effectiveness of the built-in replay buffer, which acts as an "internal demonstrator", is twofold: it accelerates learning and allows the algorithm to adapt to different tasks. Empirical data confirm the superiority of the algorithm by an average of 38.46% over other considered methods, and the most successful baseline by 28.21%, showing higher success rates and faster convergence in the point-reaching task. Since the control is performed through the robot's joints, the algorithm facilitates potential adaptation to a real robot system and construction of an obstacle avoidance task. Therefore, the algorithm has also been tested on tasks requiring following a complex dynamic trajectory and obstacle avoidance. The design of the algorithm ensures its applicability to a wide range of goal-oriented tasks, making it an easily integrated solution for real-world robotics applications.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the CONTHER paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces CONTHER, a reinforcement learning (RL) algorithm for robotic manipulation tasks. CONTHER combines a Transformer-based architecture for context understanding with Hindsight Experience Replay (HER) for addressing sparse reward scenarios. The Transformer enables the agent to consider past states and actions, while HER artificially populates the replay buffer with successful trajectories. The algorithm is tested in simulation environments with point-reaching and complex trajectory following tasks, including obstacle avoidance, demonstrating improved performance compared to baselines like TD3, TD3+HER, and TD3+Context. The paper claims that CONTHER facilitates faster convergence, higher success rates, and better generalization in real-world applications by reducing the reliance on expert demonstrations.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Combination of Techniques:** The core strength of the paper lies in combining Transformer-based context awareness with HER for sparse reward problems in robotics. While both techniques are established, their integration is presented as a novel approach.
    *   **Empirical Validation:** The paper includes experiments comparing CONTHER against several baselines in simulated robotic environments. The reported results demonstrate a quantitative improvement in success rates and convergence speed for point-reaching and trajectory-following tasks with obstacle avoidance, which are valuable.
    *   **Clear Methodology:** The paper provides a relatively clear description of the algorithm and experimental setup, enabling others to potentially reproduce the results.
    *   **Addressing a Practical Problem:** The paper tackles the practical challenge of sparse rewards in robotic RL, a critical barrier to real-world deployment.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Incremental Novelty:** While the combination of Transformers and HER is presented as novel, the core ideas behind each component are well-established. The contribution might be seen as primarily an engineering effort of integrating these techniques, rather than a fundamental theoretical breakthrough. The novelty of the algorithm's structure (v0 and v1 versions) also needs better justification of why that specific change resulted in the performance improvement.
    *   **Limited Real-World Validation:** The experiments are conducted solely in simulation. The true test of CONTHER's effectiveness would be its performance on a real robot, which is only mentioned as a potential future direction. The sim-to-real gap is a significant hurdle, and the paper doesn't provide any specific discussion or strategies for mitigating this gap.
    *   **Limited Ablation Studies:** While several baselines are compared, more extensive ablation studies could further isolate the individual contributions of the Transformer and HER components. For instance, varying the length of the context window (K) in the Transformer or changing the HER sampling strategy could provide deeper insights.
    *   **Clarity on Transformer Architecture:** The exact details of the Transformer architecture used, particularly the TrXL-I variant, aren't fully elaborated. It would be beneficial to include a diagram or more detailed description of the specific configurations and parameters employed.
    *   **Limited Baselines:** The choice of baselines can be questioned. Some of the very recent advances in offline RL or other contextual RL approaches are missing, which would further strengthen the significance of this research.

*   **Significance:** The potential significance of CONTHER lies in its ability to improve the efficiency and ease of training robots for manipulation tasks. If CONTHER could effectively reduce the need for expert demonstrations and accelerate the learning process, it could facilitate the wider adoption of RL in robotics. However, the lack of real-world validation and incremental novelty limits its current impact.

**Justification of Score:**

The paper presents an interesting integration of existing techniques, demonstrating improved performance in simulated robotic tasks. However, the limited real-world validation, and incremental novelty significantly temper its impact. The absence of real-world validation is a major drawback, as it's difficult to assess the practical applicability of the algorithm. While the paper makes some clear and useful performance improvements in the simulated domain, the lack of a theoretical contribution and the limited ablation studies further reduce its overall impact. Therefore, considering both strengths and limitations, a score of **6** seems appropriate.

**Score: 6**
- **Classification**: cs.RO
- **Score**: 6/10

### On the Limits of Applying Graph Transformers for Brain Connectome Classification
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.15902v1)
- **Authors**: Jose Lara-Rangel, Clare Heinbaugh
- **Abstract**: Brain connectomes offer detailed maps of neural connections within the brain. Recent studies have proposed novel connectome graph datasets and attempted to improve connectome classification by using graph deep learning. With recent advances demonstrating transformers' ability to model intricate relationships and outperform in various domains, this work explores their performance on the novel NeuroGraph benchmark datasets and synthetic variants derived from probabilistically removing edges to simulate noisy data. Our findings suggest that graph transformers offer no major advantage over traditional GNNs on this dataset. Furthermore, both traditional and transformer GNN models maintain accuracy even with all edges removed, suggesting that the dataset's graph structures may not significantly impact predictions. We propose further assessing NeuroGraph as a brain connectome benchmark, emphasizing the need for well-curated datasets and improved preprocessing strategies to obtain meaningful edge connections.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates the application of Graph Transformers (GTs), specifically Exphormer, to brain connectome classification tasks using the NeuroGraph benchmark datasets.  The authors compare Exphormer's performance against a ResidualGCN baseline, examining its potential advantages in modeling intricate relationships and handling noisy data.  They also generate synthetic datasets by probabilistically removing edges to assess the models' resilience. The surprising finding is that GTs offer no significant performance boost over traditional GNNs, and both model types maintain accuracy even with complete edge removal. The paper suggests that the graph structure in NeuroGraph might not be as informative as initially assumed, with models potentially relying solely on node features for prediction. The authors advocate for further evaluation of NeuroGraph as a benchmark, emphasizing the need for well-curated datasets and improved preprocessing strategies to extract meaningful edge connections.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty is somewhat limited.  While applying sparse GTs (like Exphormer) to brain connectome classification is a reasonable extension of existing work, the negative result – that GTs don't outperform GCNs – diminishes its inherent novelty.  The edge-dropping experiments and the subsequent analysis questioning the informativeness of the graph structure represent a more original contribution, but the experiment design itself isn't particularly groundbreaking. The use of attention mechanisms and a neural network also did not lead to substantial improvements in any of the experiments.

*   **Significance:** The paper's significance lies in its critical assessment of the NeuroGraph benchmark.  Identifying potential limitations in widely used datasets is valuable, as it can guide future research towards more robust and meaningful benchmarks. The observation that edge information might be less crucial than node features in NeuroGraph challenges the assumption that graph-based deep learning is inherently beneficial for these tasks. However, the paper's recommendations are quite general, needing more specific guidance on alternative preprocessing or feature extraction strategies.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Rigorous Experimentation:** The paper includes a thorough set of experiments, comparing different model configurations, incorporating attention mechanisms, and systematically varying edge-dropping probabilities. The use of multiple seeds enhances the reliability of the results.
    *   **Clear Problem Statement:** The paper clearly outlines its objective and hypothesis.
    *   **Critical Analysis:** The paper provides a critical evaluation of the results, questioning underlying assumptions about the dataset and model performance.
    *   **Well-Structured:** The paper is well-organized and easy to follow.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Positive Results:** The negative findings reduce the paper's immediate impact.  While valuable, identifying limitations is less impactful than presenting a new and effective method.
    *   **General Recommendations:**  The recommendations for future work are broad and lack specific suggestions for improved preprocessing strategies.  The paper argues against existing methodology, yet does not provide a new path for future research.
    *   **Incremental Advance:** The work is more of an incremental refinement rather than a major breakthrough.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper could influence the brain connectome classification community by prompting a more critical evaluation of existing benchmark datasets and preprocessing pipelines. It might encourage researchers to explore alternative graph representations or to focus on feature engineering to extract more meaningful edge information. The findings could also temper the enthusiasm for blindly applying complex models like GTs without considering the underlying data characteristics.

*   **Justification:** The work sheds light on an important issue, suggesting that edge information in graph structures within brain connectomes is not always necessary or beneficial. This finding is based on multiple experiments and model architectures and therefore should influence further investigation. In the end, however, the result is simply a null hypothesis with no clear guidance on new techniques or experiments for future work. The work itself relies on previously investigated methodologies of graph structures and sparse GT application and should therefore not be seen as highly influential.

**Score: 5**

The paper's negative findings and broad recommendations limit its significance. It offers useful insights into the limitations of a specific benchmark but lacks the novelty or groundbreaking results to warrant a higher score.

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 5/10

### From Structured Prompts to Open Narratives: Measuring Gender Bias in LLMs Through Open-Ended Storytelling
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.15904v1)
- **Authors**: Evan Chen, Run-Jun Zhan, Yan-Bai Lin, Hung-Hsuan Chen
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) have revolutionized natural language processing, yet concerns persist regarding their tendency to reflect or amplify social biases present in their training data. This study introduces a novel evaluation framework to uncover gender biases in LLMs, focusing on their occupational narratives. Unlike previous methods relying on structured scenarios or carefully crafted prompts, our approach leverages free-form storytelling to reveal biases embedded in the models. Systematic analyses show an overrepresentation of female characters across occupations in six widely used LLMs. Additionally, our findings reveal that LLM-generated occupational gender rankings align more closely with human stereotypes than actual labor statistics. These insights underscore the need for balanced mitigation strategies to ensure fairness while avoiding the reinforcement of new stereotypes.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "FROM STRUCTURED PROMPTS TO OPEN NARRATIVES: MEASURING GENDER BIAS IN LLMS THROUGH OPEN-ENDED STORYTELLING":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a novel framework for evaluating gender bias in Large Language Models (LLMs) by analyzing the gender distribution in free-form stories generated by the LLMs when prompted with various occupations. The authors prompt the LLMs to create a story opening and setting for characters in different occupations, and then analyze the generated stories for gender cues (names and pronouns).  They compare the gender distribution of characters in the LLM-generated stories with actual labor statistics and human perceptions of gender roles in those occupations. Their findings indicate that LLMs exhibit a tendency to overrepresent female characters, even in traditionally male-dominated fields, and that the rankings of occupations by gender ratio in LLM outputs align more closely with human stereotypes than real-world data.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies primarily in its evaluation methodology.  While prior works have explored gender bias in LLMs using structured prompts or scenario-based tasks, this paper uses a more open-ended storytelling approach to elicit and quantify bias. This allows the LLM to express any potential biases in a more organic, less constrained setting, which is a definite strength. Also, comparison of LLM generated gender ratios with both real world data and human gender perception data is a significant addition.

*   **Significance:** The paper's findings have some significance. Demonstrating that LLMs reflect or amplify societal stereotypes is important, as it highlights the potential for these models to perpetuate biased views in various applications.  Furthermore, the observation that LLMs might intentionally *overcorrect* for gender imbalances in training data, potentially creating new stereotypes, is an interesting insight.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Methodological Innovation:** The open-ended storytelling approach is a strong point.
    *   **Comprehensive Analysis:** The use of multiple LLMs and comparison to both real-world statistics and human perceptions provides a well-rounded evaluation.
    *   **Clear Presentation:** The paper is well-written and the results are presented clearly, supported by relevant figures and tables.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Scale of LLMs:** The study uses 3B and 9B parameter models along with cloud based APIs. More results and studies are required with larger LLMs to confirm this novel phenomena.
    *   **Geographic Limitation:** The study primarily uses US labor statistics. This limits the generalizability of the results to other cultures and regions where gender roles and labor force participation may differ significantly.
    *   **Binary Gender Focus:** The study only considers binary gender classifications, ignoring the complexities of gender identity and representation beyond the male/female dichotomy. This is a significant limitation that should be addressed in future research.

*   **Justification for Score:**

    While the paper offers a novel methodology and presents some interesting findings, several limitations temper its overall impact. The study's relatively small LLM size, geographic and gender binary restrictions, restrict broad conclusions about LLMs in general. While this research provides a worthwhile contribution, there is significant room for future research, to make the findings much more robust.

Score: 6

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 6/10

### Jasmine: Harnessing Diffusion Prior for Self-supervised Depth Estimation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.15905v1)
- **Authors**: Jiyuan Wang, Chunyu Lin, Cheng Guan, Lang Nie, Jing He, Haodong Li, Kang Liao, Yao Zhao
- **Abstract**: In this paper, we propose Jasmine, the first Stable Diffusion (SD)-based self-supervised framework for monocular depth estimation, which effectively harnesses SD's visual priors to enhance the sharpness and generalization of unsupervised prediction. Previous SD-based methods are all supervised since adapting diffusion models for dense prediction requires high-precision supervision. In contrast, self-supervised reprojection suffers from inherent challenges (e.g., occlusions, texture-less regions, illumination variance), and the predictions exhibit blurs and artifacts that severely compromise SD's latent priors. To resolve this, we construct a novel surrogate task of hybrid image reconstruction. Without any additional supervision, it preserves the detail priors of SD models by reconstructing the images themselves while preventing depth estimation from degradation. Furthermore, to address the inherent misalignment between SD's scale and shift invariant estimation and self-supervised scale-invariant depth estimation, we build the Scale-Shift GRU. It not only bridges this distribution gap but also isolates the fine-grained texture of SD output against the interference of reprojection loss. Extensive experiments demonstrate that Jasmine achieves SoTA performance on the KITTI benchmark and exhibits superior zero-shot generalization across multiple datasets.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "Jasmine: Harnessing Diffusion Prior for Self-Supervised Depth Estimation" introduces a novel self-supervised monocular depth estimation (SSMDE) framework based on Stable Diffusion (SD). Addressing the challenge of adapting SD models to dense prediction without high-precision supervision, Jasmine employs a "hybrid image reconstruction" (HIR) task, reconstructing real and synthetic images to preserve SD's priors while tolerating color variations. A "Scale-Shift GRU" (SSG) module is introduced to address the misalignment between SD's fixed output range and the requirements of self-supervised scale-invariant depth estimation.  Experiments demonstrate state-of-the-art performance on KITTI and superior zero-shot generalization.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:**  The paper's primary novelty lies in successfully integrating Stable Diffusion into a *self-supervised* monocular depth estimation framework.  Previous SD-based depth estimation methods were *supervised*, relying on high-quality depth labels to fine-tune the diffusion model.  The HIR task is a clever solution to the core problem of preventing the self-supervised reprojection loss from corrupting SD's pre-trained priors.  The SSG module is also a novel contribution, addressing a specific challenge in adapting diffusion models to scale-invariant depth prediction.

**Significance:**  The paper makes a significant contribution by democratizing access to the benefits of diffusion models for depth estimation. By removing the need for supervised depth labels, the approach unlocks the potential to train depth estimation models on the vast quantities of unlabeled video data. This can lead to more robust and generalizable depth estimation models, particularly for scenarios where labeled data is scarce or unavailable.  The reported results on KITTI, and especially the zero-shot generalization performance, demonstrate the potential impact of this approach.  The claim of detail preservation, while qualitatively supported by some figures, could benefit from more rigorous quantitative evaluation, perhaps through metrics specifically designed to assess image sharpness.

**Strengths:**

*   **Addresses a key limitation:** Overcomes the reliance on supervised data in SD-based depth estimation.
*   **Novel technical solutions:**  HIR task and SSG module are creative and well-motivated.
*   **Strong experimental results:** Achieves state-of-the-art performance on KITTI and demonstrates excellent zero-shot generalization.
*   **Clear writing and presentation:** The paper is generally well-written and easy to follow, although the method section does contain jargon.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Dependency on SD's existing priors:** While the method reduces reliance on explicit labels, it strongly depends on the quality of priors learned by Stable Diffusion.  This might limit its applicability to domains significantly different from SD's training data.
*   **Qualitative dependence:** the paper relies on images. However, the results show minor but insignificant differences between images, and the images have low resolutions, which make detail analysis difficult.
*   **Limited evaluation of detail preservation:** More quantitative analysis or specialized metrics could strengthen the claim of improved detail sharpness.
*   **Complexity:** The overall framework is relatively complex, involving multiple modules and training stages.

**Potential Influence:**

The paper has the potential to significantly influence the field of depth estimation. By providing a practical way to leverage the power of diffusion models without the burden of supervised data, it opens the door to new research directions in self-supervised learning and domain adaptation. Its approach might inspire other researchers to find creative ways to adapt powerful generative models for downstream tasks.

**Rigorous Rationale for the Score:**

While the integration of Stable Diffusion into a self-supervised framework is a significant step forward, the paper depends on SD's inherent assumptions. The method is technically well-executed, and results clearly indicate high performance gains. Considering all above aspects, the score reflects the impact, innovation and reliability of the presented solution.

### Text-Driven Diffusion Model for Sign Language Production
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.15914v1)
- **Authors**: Jiayi He, Xu Wang, Ruobei Zhang, Shengeng Tang, Yaxiong Wang, Lechao Cheng
- **Abstract**: We introduce the hfut-lmc team's solution to the SLRTP Sign Production Challenge. The challenge aims to generate semantically aligned sign language pose sequences from text inputs. To this end, we propose a Text-driven Diffusion Model (TDM) framework. During the training phase, TDM utilizes an encoder to encode text sequences and incorporates them into the diffusion model as conditional input to generate sign pose sequences. To guarantee the high quality and accuracy of the generated pose sequences, we utilize two key loss functions. The joint loss function L_{joint} is used to precisely measure and minimize the differences between the joint positions of the generated pose sequences and those of the ground truth. Similarly, the bone orientation loss function L_{bone} is instrumental in ensuring that the orientation of the bones in the generated poses aligns with the actual, correct orientations. In the inference stage, the TDM framework takes on a different yet equally important task. It starts with noisy sequences and, under the strict constraints of the text conditions, gradually refines and generates semantically consistent sign language pose sequences. Our carefully designed framework performs well on the sign language production task, and our solution achieves a BLEU-1 score of 20.17, placing second in the challenge.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a Text-driven Diffusion Model (TDM) framework for generating sign language pose sequences directly from text input.  This addresses the Sign Language Production (SLP) task, specifically aiming to generate semantically aligned pose sequences. The TDM uses an encoder to embed the text, integrates it into a diffusion model as a conditional input, and generates the pose sequence by denoising. The key contributions include the adaptation of a diffusion model to the text-to-pose problem, the use of a skeleton constraint loss (Lbone) alongside a joint loss (Ljoint) to enhance the accuracy of both joint positions and bone orientations, and achieving second place in the SLRTP Sign Production Challenge with a BLEU-1 score of 20.17 on the PHOENIX14T dataset.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:**  The novelty lies in the application of a diffusion model directly to the challenging text-to-pose generation task in sign language production.  While diffusion models have been used in other areas of image and video generation, the direct mapping from text to 3D pose sequences using a diffusion model is a valuable contribution. Further, the inclusion of a bone orientation loss alongside the standard joint loss is a meaningful addition to ensure anatomical plausibility and semantic correctness of the generated sign language. Previous methods typically rely on a two-step approach (text-to-gloss then gloss-to-pose) which adds complexity and potential for information loss. This direct approach is a welcome advancement.

*   **Significance:** Sign language production is a crucial area for accessibility and communication within the deaf community. By developing a method that directly translates text into sign language poses, the paper contributes to making information more accessible.  The reported performance on the PHOENIX14T dataset, while second place, showcases the potential of the TDM framework.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Clear problem definition and motivation.
    *   Well-structured explanation of the proposed TDM framework.
    *   Incorporation of bone orientation loss to improve pose accuracy.
    *   Demonstrated performance on a standard benchmark dataset.
    *   The attempt to move beyond the traditional text-to-gloss paradigm is commendable.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The paper relies on the PHOENIX14T dataset. While it is a common dataset, it is known to have limitations in the range and diversity of sign language it represents.
    *   While the bone orientation loss is conceptually good, the specific formula used (computing the direction of bone movement from parent/child nodes) might be oversimplified and not capture the full complexity of bone orientation in sign language.
    *   While the paper reports quantitative results, it would benefit from more in-depth qualitative analysis and ablation studies demonstrating the specific impact of the bone orientation loss and the choice of diffusion model components.
    *   The method is compared against a few existing studies, but it lacks a detailed comparison with state-of-the-art (SOTA) sign language production systems, thus, making the real-world impact hard to fully assess.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence the development of more accurate and natural sign language production systems. The direct text-to-pose approach and the bone orientation loss are valuable contributions that could be adopted and further improved upon by other researchers. The use of diffusion models opens up new avenues for research in this area. However, the impact depends on the reproducibility of the results, future work, and a clear demonstration of superiority over the SOTA methods.

**Justification:** The paper is novel in its approach of applying diffusion models directly to text-to-pose generation for sign language and incorporating a bone orientation loss. The experimental results, while not groundbreaking, are promising and demonstrate the potential of the TDM framework. However, the limited diversity of the dataset, and need for more comprehensive experimental evaluation hold the paper back. A score of 6 reflects the positive contributions and limitations.

**Score: 6**

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 6/10

### Towards Automatic Continual Learning: A Self-Adaptive Framework for Continual Instruction Tuning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.15924v1)
- **Authors**: Peiyi Lin, Fukai Zhang, Kai Niu, Hao Fu
- **Abstract**: Continual instruction tuning enables large language models (LLMs) to learn incrementally while retaining past knowledge, whereas existing methods primarily focus on how to retain old knowledge rather than on selecting which new knowledge to learn. In domain-specific contexts, maintaining data quality and managing system constraints remain key challenges. To address these issues, we propose an automated continual instruction tuning framework that dynamically filters incoming data, which identify and reduce redundant data across successive updates. Our approach utilizes a small proxy model for efficient perplexity-based filtering, and updates the proxy to ensure that the filtering criteria remain aligned with the evolving state of the deployed model. Compared to existing static data selection methods, our framework can effectively handle incrementally acquired data and shifting distributions. Additionally, it addresses practical deployment challenges by enabling seamless model updates, supporting version rollback and incorporating automatic checkpoint evaluation. We evaluated the system in real-world medical scenarios. It reduced computational costs by 66.7% and improved model performance, and achieved autonomous updates, thus demonstrating its effectiveness for automatic continual instruction tuning.
- **Summary**: Okay, here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Towards Automatic Continual Learning: A Self-Adaptive Framework for Continual Instruction Tuning."

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the challenges of continual instruction tuning for large language models (LLMs), particularly in domain-specific contexts. It proposes an automated framework that dynamically filters incoming data to maintain quality and reduce redundancy across successive updates. The core idea is to use a small proxy model to efficiently filter data based on perplexity and iteratively update the proxy model to align with the evolving deployed model.  The framework also incorporates system-level features for practical deployment, such as seamless model updates, version rollback, and automatic checkpoint evaluation. The system is evaluated in a real-world medical scenario, showing reductions in computational cost and improvements in model performance.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:**

The paper's novelty lies in its holistic approach to continual instruction tuning, combining dynamic data filtering with practical deployment considerations. While individual components, such as perplexity-based filtering and the use of a proxy model, have been explored in previous work, the integration of these elements into a self-adaptive framework, along with deployment features such as seamless updates and automatic checkpoint evaluation, provides a unique contribution. The concept of continuously updating the proxy model alongside the main model to address shifting data distributions is also a significant improvement over static data selection methods.

**Significance:**

The significance of the paper stems from its potential to address real-world challenges in deploying and maintaining continually learning LLMs in domain-specific applications. The framework's ability to automate data filtering, reduce computational costs, and ensure smooth updates is highly valuable for scenarios where manual intervention is limited, or data is constantly evolving.  The evaluation in a medical setting further enhances its significance, showcasing its applicability to a sensitive and critical domain. The improvements in model performance, coupled with reduced computational cost, present a compelling argument for the framework's effectiveness.

**Strengths:**

*   **Holistic Approach:** The paper addresses both data quality and deployment concerns, providing a more complete solution for continual instruction tuning.
*   **Dynamic Data Filtering:** The self-adaptive data filtering framework is a strong contribution, enabling the system to handle incrementally acquired data and shifting distributions.
*   **Practical Deployment Features:** The inclusion of seamless updates, version rollback, and automatic checkpoint evaluation enhances the framework's practicality and usability.
*   **Real-World Evaluation:** The evaluation in a medical scenario adds credibility and demonstrates the framework's applicability to real-world problems.
*   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly identifies and articulates the challenges of continual instruction tuning in domain-specific contexts.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Novelty of Individual Components:** Some components, like perplexity-based filtering and the proxy model approach, have been explored in previous work. The novelty relies on the integration and self-adaptive nature.
*   **Scope of Evaluation:** While the medical scenario is valuable, the evaluation could be strengthened by including other domain-specific tasks and datasets.
*   **Hyperparameter Sensitivity:** The performance of the framework likely depends on the choice of hyperparameters, such as the thresholds for length, diversity, and IFD score. The paper could benefit from a more detailed discussion of how these parameters were selected and their impact on performance.
*   **Comparative Evaluation:** While the paper compares against using the full dataset, it would be beneficial to compare against other dynamic data selection strategies, if available.
*   **Scalability:** The paper does not provide an explicit discussion about the systems capability to scale with extremely larger datasets.

**Justification of Score:**

The paper offers a significant contribution to the field by providing a practical and automated framework for continual instruction tuning of LLMs. The self-adaptive data filtering, coupled with system-level deployment features, addresses critical challenges in real-world applications. While some components are based on existing techniques, the integration and continuous adaptation of the proxy model are novel and effective. However, the impact would be strengthened by the issues raised in weaknesses of this paper, namely more evaluation of other data selection strategies and more discussion on scalability.

**Score: 7.5**

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### BlockDance: Reuse Structurally Similar Spatio-Temporal Features to Accelerate Diffusion Transformers
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.15927v1)
- **Authors**: Hui Zhang, Tingwei Gao, Jie Shao, Zuxuan Wu
- **Abstract**: Diffusion models have demonstrated impressive generation capabilities, particularly with recent advancements leveraging transformer architectures to improve both visual and artistic quality. However, Diffusion Transformers (DiTs) continue to encounter challenges related to low inference speed, primarily due to the iterative denoising process. To address this issue, we propose BlockDance, a training-free approach that explores feature similarities at adjacent time steps to accelerate DiTs. Unlike previous feature-reuse methods that lack tailored reuse strategies for features at different scales, BlockDance prioritizes the identification of the most structurally similar features, referred to as Structurally Similar Spatio-Temporal (STSS) features. These features are primarily located within the structure-focused blocks of the transformer during the later stages of denoising. BlockDance caches and reuses these highly similar features to mitigate redundant computation, thereby accelerating DiTs while maximizing consistency with the generated results of the original model. Furthermore, considering the diversity of generated content and the varying distributions of redundant features, we introduce BlockDance-Ada, a lightweight decision-making network tailored for instance-specific acceleration. BlockDance-Ada dynamically allocates resources and provides superior content quality. Both BlockDance and BlockDance-Ada have proven effective across various generation tasks and models, achieving accelerations between 25% and 50% while maintaining generation quality.
- **Summary**: Okay, I will provide a concise summary and a critical evaluation of the paper "BlockDance: Reuse Structurally Similar Spatio-Temporal Features to Accelerate Diffusion Transformers," based on the OCR output.

**Summary:**

The paper proposes "BlockDance," a training-free approach to accelerate Diffusion Transformers (DiTs) by reusing structurally similar features at adjacent time steps during the denoising process. It identifies "Structurally Similar Spatio-Temporal (STSS) features," primarily located in the structure-focused blocks of the transformer in the later stages of denoising. BlockDance caches and reuses these features to reduce redundant computation. The paper introduces BlockDance-Ada, a lightweight decision-making network based on reinforcement learning, which dynamically allocates resources for instance-specific acceleration based on the prompt and intermediate latent.  The authors demonstrate the efficacy of the method across various generation tasks and models (DiT-XL/2, PixArt-a, and Open-Sora), achieving acceleration between 25% and 50% while maintaining generation quality.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in its *targeted* feature reuse strategy specific to DiTs. Existing methods often perform coarse-grained feature reuse, leading to structural distortions. BlockDance, by focusing on structurally similar features in the later denoising stages after structure stabilization, avoids the pitfalls of generic feature reuse. The introduction of the reinforcement learning-based adaptive allocation (BlockDance-Ada) adds another layer of novelty by enabling instance-specific optimization. The idea of splitting the process to cache step and reuse step and focusing on later stages of denoising is an important observation leading to innovation in the research of DiTs.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a critical bottleneck in diffusion models: the slow inference speed due to the iterative denoising process. Accelerating DiTs without significant quality degradation is a significant contribution. The experimental results across different models and tasks suggest the method's generalizability and potential impact on the broader diffusion model research area. By providing a training-free method to accelerate inference the solution becomes more applicable to the broad spectrum of DiT models and usage. Also, the paper provides an innovative insight to how information evolves during the image generating process through denoising.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Training-free approach:** The method doesn't require retraining, making it readily applicable to existing pre-trained DiT models.
    *   **Targeted Feature Reuse:** Strategically reusing features from the shallower layers once structural stability has been established makes the process much more efficient.
    *   **Adaptive allocation (BlockDance-Ada):**  The reinforcement learning framework allows instance-specific optimization, further improving efficiency and quality.
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The method is evaluated on multiple models (DiT, PixArt-a, Open-Sora), datasets (ImageNet, COCO, MSR-VTT), and tasks (class-conditional generation, text-to-image, text-to-video).
    *   **Clear Presentation:** The paper clearly explains the proposed approach and its advantages over existing methods. Figures are useful in visualizing the process.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited benefits on models with very few denoising steps:** The paper acknowledges that the benefits are limited in scenarios with very few denoising steps.
    *   **Complexity:** The reliance on a reinforcement learning agent to make the step-by-step decision on cache or reuse might increase complexity and overhead compared to the non-adaptive BlockDance, especially if it requires significant tuning to learn the optimal reuse policies.
    *   **Reproducibility:** The paper provides limited details on the implementation and training of the reinforcement learning aspect of BlockDance-Ada, potentially hindering reproducibility.
    *   **Adaptive component's generalizability.** BlockDance-Ada uses prompt and the intermediate latent variable of the model to guide the decision-making, which may influence the generalizability of the adaptive component of the model.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper is likely to influence future research on accelerating diffusion models. The idea of targeted feature reuse based on structural similarity and the use of reinforcement learning for adaptive resource allocation are promising directions for further exploration. The identification of structure stabilization during denoising is useful in further exploration of different architectures for DiT.

**Justification of Score:**

I am assigning a score of **7**. The paper is above average with respect to novelty, significance, and potential influence. The core concept of reusing similar spatio-temporal features, particularly focusing on the later stages of denoising after structure stabilization is innovative and well-motivated. The results are demonstrated through a range of experiments. However, the limited improvement on DiTs that have few denoising steps and implementation complexities due to the reliance on reinforcement learning, restricts the rating of this paper.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### SaMam: Style-aware State Space Model for Arbitrary Image Style Transfer
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.15934v1)
- **Authors**: Hongda Liu, Longguang Wang, Ye Zhang, Ziru Yu, Yulan Guo
- **Abstract**: Global effective receptive field plays a crucial role for image style transfer (ST) to obtain high-quality stylized results. However, existing ST backbones (e.g., CNNs and Transformers) suffer huge computational complexity to achieve global receptive fields. Recently, the State Space Model (SSM), especially the improved variant Mamba, has shown great potential for long-range dependency modeling with linear complexity, which offers a approach to resolve the above dilemma. In this paper, we develop a Mamba-based style transfer framework, termed SaMam. Specifically, a mamba encoder is designed to efficiently extract content and style information. In addition, a style-aware mamba decoder is developed to flexibly adapt to various styles. Moreover, to address the problems of local pixel forgetting, channel redundancy and spatial discontinuity of existing SSMs, we introduce both local enhancement and zigzag scan. Qualitative and quantitative results demonstrate that our SaMam outperforms state-of-the-art methods in terms of both accuracy and efficiency.
- **Summary**: Okay, I will provide a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "SaMam: Style-aware State Space Model for Arbitrary Image Style Transfer."

**Summary:**

The paper introduces SaMam, a novel image style transfer framework based on the Mamba state space model. The authors address the trade-off between global receptive field and computational complexity in style transfer, which is a known issue with existing CNN- and Transformer-based methods. SaMam employs a Mamba encoder to extract content and style information, a style-aware Mamba decoder to adapt to different styles using style embeddings, and a zigzag scan mechanism to enhance spatial continuity. The authors demonstrate that SaMam achieves state-of-the-art performance in terms of accuracy and efficiency through qualitative and quantitative experiments. The key innovations include the Mamba-based architecture, the style-aware selective scan structured state space sequence block (S7 block), and the zigzag scan method.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The novelty of this paper lies primarily in the application of the Mamba architecture to the task of arbitrary image style transfer. While Mamba has been explored in other vision tasks, its adaptation to style transfer with specific architectural modifications like the S7 block and the zigzag scan is a contribution. The style-aware Mamba decoder design is also a novel element. However, each component can be seen as an extension of previous research. The core Mamba idea is from Gu et al, LoE is from the prior paper. The novelty is a collection of many components, each of which is not novel by itself.
*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a relevant problem in style transfer – the computational cost associated with achieving a global receptive field. By leveraging Mamba's linear complexity for long-range dependency modeling, SaMam presents a potentially more efficient alternative to existing methods. The reported improvements in both accuracy and efficiency, if validated, could influence the design of future style transfer frameworks. In terms of performance, the improvement is not significant enough to justify the introduction of this new approach, although the efficiency gain is noticeable.

**Strengths:**

*   **Clear problem definition:** The paper clearly articulates the limitations of existing style transfer backbones (CNNs and Transformers).
*   **Well-defined approach:** The SaMam architecture and its components (encoder, style-aware decoder, S7 block, zigzag scan) are well-defined and explained.
*   **Experimental validation:** The paper presents both qualitative and quantitative results to demonstrate the effectiveness of SaMam, comparing it against state-of-the-art methods.
*   **Efficiency gains:** The reported reduction in computational complexity and inference time is a significant advantage.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Incremental novelty:** While the application of Mamba to style transfer is novel, the individual components of the architecture (LoE, zigzag scan) are inspired by or adapted from existing techniques. The novelty is in the integration, but not the individual components by themselves.
*   **Limited novelty in individual building blocks**: Some building blocks are quite similar to the existing work.
*   **Missing explanation and source code:** The paper does not provide source code for validation.

**Potential Influence:**

If the performance and efficiency claims are validated, SaMam could encourage further research into applying state space models to other image processing tasks and style transfer variants. The architectural modifications introduced in SaMam (S7 block, zigzag scan) could also inspire new designs. However, the impact will depend on the community's ability to reproduce the results and the emergence of even more efficient or accurate style transfer methods.

**Justification for Score:**

Given the incremental novelty and the demonstrated gains in efficiency and performance, but also the weakness mentioned above, a score of 6/10 seems appropriate. The work is a solid engineering effort that builds upon recent advances in state space models and style transfer. It offers practical benefits in terms of computational cost and speed, but its impact on the broader field might be limited due to the incremental nature of the innovations. The use of Mamba is interesting, but the lack of significant innovation within the architecture itself and the lack of source code temper the enthusiasm.
